I’ll be listening, but I lose the thread pretty easily. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having trouble answering simple questions clearly. Like at work, someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, and I start talking about invoices, then somehow garden soil, then mortgage papers… and I can see they’re not following me. I’m not trying to do it, but my thoughts just kind of jump around.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with answering questions clearly causing you problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s starting to affect work. I’ve had to cut back on custom pieces because I miss repair deadlines or lose track when I’m talking with clients. And invoices—I used to handle those myself, but now I have my husband double-check them because I don’t trust that I’ve followed everything correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected or hard to follow even when you're not trying to answer questions or explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I’m not explaining something, my thoughts can feel like they jump tracks. Like I’ll be thinking about one thing, and then suddenly I’m on invoices, then the garden, then mortgage papers, and I don’t always know how I got there. It’s harder when someone asks me a simple question, because then they can actually hear it happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear what sounds like a woman muttering my name from the polishing room. And sometimes I smell burning metal even when the torches are off. It feels real enough that I usually go check.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when you're speaking, even when you're not trying to explain something complicated?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even with simple things, my thoughts don’t stay lined up. Someone will ask me when a ring repair will be ready, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about invoices, or garden soil, or mortgage papers somehow. I can see the person getting lost, and then I realize I’m not really answering what they asked.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following a conversation when you're not the one talking?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. I’ll be listening, but I lose the thread pretty easily. It’s a lot like when I’m the one answering—someone asks me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and my mind goes off to invoices, garden soil, mortgage papers… and then I can tell I’m not really with them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to complete everyday tasks because your thoughts are disorganized?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can make simple tasks harder. Like at the shop, someone asks me when a ring repair will be ready, and I start trying to answer, but my mind jumps to invoices, then garden soil, then mortgage papers. By the time I notice, I’m not really answering them anymore, and I can see they’re losing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel more energetic or restless than usual?\n\nPatient: Not more energetic, really. If anything, my husband says I move slower at the bench now, like even picking up tweezers takes effort. But when I’m anxious, I do get restless with my hands—I’ll rub my fingers together until they’re sore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to make decisions in your daily life because of your thoughts jumping around?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even small decisions can get tangled up. Someone might ask me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and instead of just deciding what to say, my mind jumps to invoices, then garden soil, then mortgage papers. Then I can see the person sort of losing me, and I realize I’m not answering the question clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than you intend to, even when it's not necessary?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I might ramble a little if I’m nervous, but I haven’t noticed myself talking a lot more than I mean to or feeling like I can’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself speaking faster than usual?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed myself speaking faster than usual. Maybe if I’m nervous I stumble a bit, but I don’t think my speech gets especially fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're speaking without being able to stop?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I might lose my train of thought sometimes, but I don’t usually feel like words are just coming out and I can’t stop them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big problem for me. It’s starting to affect work. I’ve had to cut back on custom pieces because I miss repair deadlines or lose track when I’m talking with clients. And invoices—I used to handle those myself, but now I have my husband double-check them because I don’t trust that I’ve followed everything correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected or hard to follow even when you're not trying to answer questions or explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I’m not explaining something, my thoughts can feel like they jump tracks. Like I’ll be thinking about one thing, and then suddenly I’m on invoices, then the garden, then mortgage papers, and I don’t always know how I got there. It’s harder when someone asks me a simple question, because then they can actually hear it happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear what sounds like a woman muttering my name from the polishing room. And sometimes I smell burning metal even when the torches are off. Even with simple things, my thoughts don’t stay lined up. I’ll be listening, but I lose the thread pretty easily. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23.0, F90.0, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having trouble answering simple questions clearly. Like at work, someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, and I start talking about invoices, then somehow garden soil, then mortgage papers… and I can see they’re not following me. I’m not trying to do it, but my thoughts just kind of jump around.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with answering questions clearly causing you problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s starting to affect work. I’ve had to cut back on custom pieces because I miss repair deadlines or lose track when I’m talking with clients. And invoices—I used to handle those myself, but now I have my husband double-check them because I don’t trust that I’ve followed everything correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected or hard to follow even when you're not trying to answer questions or explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I’m not explaining something, my thoughts can feel like they jump tracks. Like I’ll be thinking about one thing, and then suddenly I’m on invoices, then the garden, then mortgage papers, and I don’t always know how I got there. It’s harder when someone asks me a simple question, because then they can actually hear it happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear what sounds like a woman muttering my name from the polishing room. And sometimes I smell burning metal even when the torches are off. It feels real enough that I usually go check.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when you're speaking, even when you're not trying to explain something complicated?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even with simple things, my thoughts don’t stay lined up. Someone will ask me when a ring repair will be ready, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about invoices, or garden soil, or mortgage papers somehow. I can see the person getting lost, and then I realize I’m not really answering what they asked.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following a conversation when you're not the one talking?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. I’ll be listening, but I lose the thread pretty easily. It’s a lot like when I’m the one answering—someone asks me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and my mind goes off to invoices, garden soil, mortgage papers… and then I can tell I’m not really with them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to complete everyday tasks because your thoughts are disorganized?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can make simple tasks harder. Like at the shop, someone asks me when a ring repair will be ready, and I start trying to answer, but my mind jumps to invoices, then garden soil, then mortgage papers. By the time I notice, I’m not really answering them anymore, and I can see they’re losing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel more energetic or restless than usual?\n\nPatient: Not more energetic, really. If anything, my husband says I move slower at the bench now, like even picking up tweezers takes effort. But when I’m anxious, I do get restless with my hands—I’ll rub my fingers together until they’re sore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to make decisions in your daily life because of your thoughts jumping around?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even small decisions can get tangled up. Someone might ask me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and instead of just deciding what to say, my mind jumps to invoices, then garden soil, then mortgage papers. Then I can see the person sort of losing me, and I realize I’m not answering the question clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than you intend to, even when it's not necessary?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I might ramble a little if I’m nervous, but I haven’t noticed myself talking a lot more than I mean to or feeling like I can’t stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself speaking faster than usual?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed myself speaking faster than usual. Maybe if I’m nervous I stumble a bit, but I don’t think my speech gets especially fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're speaking without being able to stop?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I might lose my train of thought sometimes, but I don’t usually feel like words are just coming out and I can’t stop them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big problem for me. I usually try to think things through, especially with money or work. I might make a quick decision sometimes, but not in a way that gets me into trouble, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’m especially distracted by things around me. I can usually stay with what I’m doing, unless there’s a lot going on or I’m already tired.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when your thoughts jump around and you have trouble answering questions clearly?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get stressed when that happens. That’s actually part of why I came in. Someone can ask me something simple at work, like when a ring repair will be ready, and I start answering but then I’m suddenly talking about invoices, then garden soil, then mortgage papers. I can see on their face that I’m not making sense, and that makes me anxious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are more disorganized than your speech, or is it the other way around?\n\nPatient: I think it starts in my thoughts, and then it comes out in my speech. Like, I’m trying to answer one simple thing, but my mind jumps to something else, and then I say that out loud. That’s why I came in — someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, and suddenly I’m talking about invoices or garden soil or mortgage papers, and I can tell they’re not following me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having trouble answering simple questions clearly. Like at work, someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, and I start talking about invoices, then somehow garden soil, then mortgage papers… and I can see they’re not following me. I’m not trying to do it, but my thoughts just kind of jump around.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with answering questions clearly causing you problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s starting to affect work. I’ve had to cut back on custom pieces because I miss repair deadlines or lose track when I’m talking with clients. And invoices—I used to handle those myself, but now I have my husband double-check them because I don’t trust that I’ve followed everything correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected or hard to follow even when you're not trying to answer questions or explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I’m not explaining something, my thoughts can feel like they jump tracks. Like I’ll be thinking about one thing, and then suddenly I’m on invoices, then the garden, then mortgage papers, and I don’t always know how I got there. It’s harder when someone asks me a simple question, because then they can actually hear it happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear what sounds like a woman muttering my name from the polishing room. And sometimes I smell burning metal even when the torches are off. It feels real enough that I usually go check.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when you're speaking, even when you're not trying to explain something complicated?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even with simple things, my thoughts don’t stay lined up. Someone will ask me when a ring repair will be ready, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about invoices, or garden soil, or mortgage papers somehow. I can see the person getting lost, and then I realize I’m not really answering what they asked.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following a conversation when you're not the one talking?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. I’ll be listening, but I lose the thread pretty easily. It’s a lot like when I’m the one answering—someone asks me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and my mind goes off to invoices, garden soil, mortgage papers… and then I can tell I’m not really with them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to complete everyday tasks because your thoughts are disorganized?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can make simple tasks harder. Like at the shop, someone asks me when a ring repair will be ready, and I start trying to answer, but my mind jumps to invoices, then garden soil, then mortgage papers. By the time I notice, I’m not really answering them anymore, and I can see they’re losing me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel more energetic or restless than usual?\n\nPatient: Not more energetic, really. If anything, my husband says I move slower at the bench now, like even picking up tweezers takes effort. Someone might ask me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and instead of just deciding what to say, my mind jumps to invoices, then garden soil, then mortgage papers. I can see they’re not following me, and that worries me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often hear or see things that aren't there when you're not in a quiet or stressful situation?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 53 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"When the shop is quiet I sometimes hear a woman mutter my name from the polishing room or smell burning metal when the torches are off, and it feels real enough that I go check.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

I’ll be listening, but I lose the thread pretty easily. It’s a lot like when I’m the one answering—someone asks me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and my mind goes off to invoices, garden soil, mortgage papers… and then I can tell I’m not really with them anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to complete everyday tasks because your thoughts are disorganized?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can make simple tasks harder. Like at the shop, someone asks me when a ring repair will be ready, and I start trying to answer, but my mind jumps to invoices, then garden soil, then mortgage papers.
